\int_{V}\boldsymbol{E}^{2}d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}\omega_{k}^{2}V\left[A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{*}+A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{*}A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}\right],

(219)

and thus, the total energy is

H=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\varepsilon_{0}V\omega_{k}^{2}\left[A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{*}+A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{*}A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}\right].

(220)

Which corresponds to a sum of the energy over the several modes of
the system. Note that we did not commute and add together the complex
numbers
A_{k}^{*}A_{k}
precisely because this brings out a fortuitous
comparison that we can make with the energy levels of a harmonic oscillator.
This gives us a hint toward the following considerations we can make
for the quantization of the E-M fields. The fundamental idea is that
to each mode of the radiation field, we associate a harmonic oscillator.
The degree of excitation of this harmonic oscillator gives us the
number of photons that occupy a mode. Thus, the term photon acquires
a precise definition in this context. A photon is an excitation of
the harmonic oscillator associated with a mode of the E-M fields.
The Hamiltonian for a harmonic oscillator is

\hat{H}_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\hbar\omega_{k}\left(\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{\dagger}+\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}\right).

(221)

If we associate one such Hamiltonian to a mode, then the total Hamiltonian
will of course be the sum over the modes of each individual Hamiltonian

\hat{H}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{H}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\frac{1}{2}\hbar\omega_{k}\left(\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{\dagger}+\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}\right).

(222)

Note the striking similarity with the equation (
220
)
for the classical energy stored in the E-M field in the volume
V
.
We can in fact make a simple correspondence between equations (
222
)
and (
220
) by introducing the quantization
rules

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}\rightarrow\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2\varepsilon_{0}V\omega_{k}}}\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda},\\
A_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{*}\rightarrow\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2\varepsilon_{0}V\omega_{k}}}\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k},\lambda}^{\dagger}.\end{array}\right.

(223)

For notation’s sake we compress both indices
\boldsymbol{k}
and

\lambda
into a single
\boldsymbol{k}
. Thus, for each mode, the
creation operator
\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}
acts by creating
a photon in this mode, while the destruction operator acts by removing
a photon from the mode

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}\left|n_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n_{\boldsymbol{k}}+1}\left|n_{\boldsymbol{k}}+1\right\rangle,

(224)

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\left|n_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\left|n_{\boldsymbol{k}}-1\right\rangle.

(225)

Also, it is possible to measure how many photons occupy each mode
by acting with the number operator
\hat{n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}},

since

\hat{n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\left|n_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle=n_{\boldsymbol{k}}\left|n_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right\rangle.

(226)

These states
\left|n_{k}\right\rangle
with a precisely defined
photon number are called Fock states but we may also consider states
which are superpositions of these states an thus do not have a defined
number of photons.

Since we have determined the quantization rules which give us the
creation and annihilation operators for the radiation field, we may
now construct the operator which corresponds to the vector potential

\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(\boldsymbol{r},t)=\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2\varepsilon_{0}V\omega_{k}}}\left[\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}e^{i\left(\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-\omega_{k}t\right)}+\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}e^{-i\left(\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-\omega_{k}t\right)}\right].

(227)

From this operator we may also construct the field operators that
correspond to the electric and magnetic field by using equations (
205
).
We have for a single mode

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\hat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(\boldsymbol{r},t)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}(\boldsymbol{r},t)=i\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar\omega_{k}}{2\varepsilon_{0}V}}\left[\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}e^{i\left(\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-\omega_{k}t\right)}-\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}e^{-i\left(\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-\omega_{k}t\right)}\right],\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(\boldsymbol{r},t)=\nabla\times\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}(\boldsymbol{r},t)=i\left(\boldsymbol{k}\times\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2\varepsilon_{0}V\omega_{k}}}\left[\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}e^{i\left(\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-\omega_{k}t\right)}-\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}e^{-i\left(\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-\omega_{k}t\right)}\right].\end{array}\right.

(228)

Of course, the total operators are given by a sum over the modes

\hat{\boldsymbol{E}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}},

(229)

and note also, that quite generally, these field operators can be
decomposed into parts which oscillate with positive and negative frequencies
as

\hat{\boldsymbol{E}}(\boldsymbol{r},t)=\hat{\boldsymbol{E}}^{+}(\boldsymbol{r},t)+\hat{\boldsymbol{E}}^{-}(\boldsymbol{r},t).

(230)

Using the quantization rules, one has that the photon Hamiltonian
is given by

\hat{H}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hbar\omega_{k}\left(\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+1/2\right),

(231)

which acts on a general Fock state with many modes (multi-mode Fock
state) as

\hat{H}\left|n_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}},n_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}},\ldots\right\rangle=\left[\hbar\omega_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\left(n_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}+1/2\right)+\hbar\omega_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}\left(n_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}+1/2\right)+\cdots\right]\left|n_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}},n_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}},\ldots\right\rangle

(232)

In general we shall, in the main text and following appendices work
with thermal states, which are indeed multi-mode Fock states with
a probability distribution given by the canonical distribution.

Appendix D
Two-level systems

In this appendix we go over a few useful properties of a two-level
system (two-state atom or qubit), with states
|e\rangle,|g\rangle

characterized by energies
E_{e}
and
E_{g}
respectively. The
energy
E_{e}
corresponds to the excited state while
E_{g}
corresponds
to the ground-state. Of course the states are labeled such that
E_{e}>E_{g}
.
We will make use of the Pauli matrices as in particular, the system
Hamiltonian for a two-level system is of course

H_{S}=E_{e}|e\rangle\langle e|+E_{g}|g\rangle\langle g|,

(233)

which can be written in a more convenient form as

H_{S}=\frac{1}{2}\left(E_{e}+E_{g}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(E_{e}-E_{g}\right)\sigma_{z},

(234)

where the Pauli matrix
\sigma_{z}
corresponds to

\sigma_{z}=|e\rangle\langle e|-|g\rangle\langle g|.

(235)

Choosing the energy reference so that the zero is in the middle of
the energies
E_{e}
and
E_{g}.
In this case the Hamiltonian is
just

H_{S}=\frac{1}{2}\left(E_{e}-E_{g}\right)\sigma_{z},

(236)

which we can write as

H_{S}=\frac{1}{2}\hbar\omega_{0}\sigma_{z}.

(237)

We may want to describe the light-matter interaction for this two-level
system. To do so we need to write the dipole operator
q\boldsymbol{r}

conveniently, and as such, if we introduce the frequency
\omega_{0}=\left(E_{e}-E_{g}\right)/\hbar
,
we note that it can be expanded in terms of a complete basis of the
system’s Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{S}

q\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}=q\sum_{ij}\langle i|\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}|j\rangle|i\rangle\langle j|.

(238)

In the case of the two-level system this reduces to

q\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}=q(\langle e|\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}|g\rangle|e\rangle\langle g|+\langle g|\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}|e\rangle|g\rangle\langle e|),

(239)

and thus can define the dipole operators

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}=q\langle g|\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}|e\rangle,\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{eg}=q\langle e|\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}|g\rangle=\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}^{*}.\end{array}\right.

(240)

With this notation we write

q\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}=\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}\sigma_{-}+\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}^{*}\sigma_{+}.

(241)

It may be also worth noting that the expectation value of the dipole
operator has a particular simple realization in the density operator
formalism. It is given by

\displaystyle\langle q\hat{r}\rangle

\displaystyle=\text{tr}\{\hat{\rho}q\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}\}=\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}\text{tr}\left\{\hat{\rho}\sigma_{-}\right\}+\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}^{*}\text{tr}\left\{\hat{\rho}\sigma_{+}\right\}

(242)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}\langle e|\hat{\rho}|g\rangle+\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}^{*}\langle g|\hat{\rho}|e\rangle

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}\rho_{eg}+\boldsymbol{d}_{ge}^{*}\rho_{ge}.

Of use in our considerations are also the identities

\left[\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-}\right]=\sigma_{z},

(243)

\left[\sigma_{\pm},\sigma_{z}\right]=\mp 2\sigma_{\pm},

(244)

\sigma_{\pm}\sigma_{\mp}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathds{1}\pm\sigma_{z}\right).

(245)

These will be used throughout the text very often, for instance the
last equation (
245
) represents in the case of
a two-level system, a freedom in the choice of the zero of the energy.
If we choose the zero to be exactly in the middle between the excited
and ground states, the Hamiltonian will read
H=\hbar\omega_{0}\sigma_{z}/2
,
where
\hbar\omega_{0}
is the energy gap between excited and ground
state. If we make the zero of the energy coincide with the ground
state, then we can write
H=\hbar\omega_{0}\sigma_{+}\sigma_{-}/2
.
Both these representations for two-level atoms are used throughout
the paper, and equation (
245
) is useful as a transition
between them.

Appendix E
The Sokhotski-Plemelj identity

This appendix is dedicated to obtaining a result that is used again
and again throughout the paper. It is sometimes referred to as the
Sokhotski-Plemelj theorem or identity, and states that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}e^{-i(\omega_{0}-\omega)\left(t_{1}-t\right)}

\displaystyle=\pi\delta(\omega-\omega_{0})-i\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{0}}.

(246)

For completeness, we prove this identity here, noting again that we
will often employ it. We start by performing a change of variables

\tau=t-t_{1}
and write

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}e^{-i(\omega_{0}-\omega)\left(t_{1}-t\right)}

\displaystyle=-\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{t}^{0}d\tau e^{i(\omega_{0}-\omega)\tau}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau e^{i(\omega_{0}-\omega)\tau}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\tau e^{i(\omega_{0}-\omega)\tau}.

(247)

This integral can be regularized by adding a small imaginary part

-i\varepsilon
to the frequency
\omega
, and solving the integral
before letting it go to 0.

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{0}^{t}d\tau e^{i(\omega_{0}-\omega+i\varepsilon)\tau}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{-1}{i(\omega_{0}-\omega+i\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\omega_{0}-\omega+i\eta},

(248)

where
\eta=0^{+}
is written so as to omit the limit. This form
of writing the integral is often utilized, but note that it is merely
a formal expression. It is in fact a distribution, and makes sense
only inside an integral. We can manipulate it as

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\omega_{0}-\omega+i\eta}

\displaystyle=i\frac{1}{\omega_{0}-\omega+i\eta}

\displaystyle=i\left(\frac{\omega_{0}-\omega}{(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}}-\frac{i\eta}{(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\pi\frac{\eta}{\pi\left[(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}\right]}+i\frac{\omega_{0}-\omega}{(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}}.

(249)

We now can calculate the following quantity

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega f(\omega)\frac{i}{\omega_{0}-\omega+i\eta}

\displaystyle=\pi\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{\eta}{\pi\left[(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}\right]}f(\omega)+i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{\left(\omega_{0}-\omega\right)^{2}}{(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\frac{f(\omega)}{\omega_{0}-\omega}.

(250)

For the first integral we find, when
\omega\to\omega_{0}
it approaches

\infty
and when
\omega\neq\omega_{0}
, approaches
0
. Additionally,
since

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{\eta}{\pi\left[(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}\right]}=1,

this is a nascent
\delta-
function, which means

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega f(\omega)\frac{i}{\omega_{0}-\omega+i\eta}=\pi\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega f(\omega)\delta(\omega-\omega_{0}).

(251)

For the second integral, when
\omega\to\omega_{0}
, that when
\omega_{0}-\omega\gg\eta
,
we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{\left(\omega_{0}-\omega\right)^{2}}{(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\frac{f(\omega)}{\omega_{0}-\omega}

\displaystyle\approx\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{\left(\omega_{0}-\omega\right)^{2}}{(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}}\frac{f(\omega)}{\omega_{0}-\omega}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{f(\omega)}{\omega_{0}-\omega},

(252)

but when
\omega_{0}-\omega\ll\eta
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{\left(\omega_{0}-\omega\right)^{2}}{(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\frac{f(\omega)}{\omega_{0}-\omega}

\displaystyle\approx\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{\left(\omega_{0}-\omega\right)}{\eta^{2}}f(\omega)

\displaystyle\to 0.

(253)

where the last line of equation (
253
) follows from
the fact that
(\omega_{0}-\omega)/\eta^{2}\to 0
. As such, we have

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{\left(\omega_{0}-\omega\right)^{2}}{(\omega_{0}-\omega)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\frac{f(\omega)}{\omega_{0}-\omega}=\mathcal{P}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{f(\omega)}{\omega_{0}-\omega},

(254)

and by putting the results together we have proved the Sokhotski-Plemelj
theorem, that states

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}e^{-i(\omega_{0}-\omega)\left(t_{1}-t\right)}

\displaystyle=\pi\delta(\omega_{0}-\omega)+i\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega_{0}-\omega}

\displaystyle=\pi\delta(\omega-\omega_{0})-i\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{0}}.

(255)

Thus, we conclude this section, introducing the notation

\mathcal{I}_{SP}(\omega)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}e^{-i(\omega_{0}-\omega)\left(t_{1}-t\right)}=\pi\delta(\omega-\omega_{0})-i\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{0}}.

(256)

and noting that we shall often call upon this result.

Appendix F
Details on the calculation of the Green’s function

F.1
The idea behind Green’s functions

We start this appendix with a few considerations and a refresher on
the Green’s function method for ODEs following the approach of
Kirkwood 2011
.
We discuss how to solve a non homogeneous linear second-order ordinary
differential equation with given boundary conditions by presenting
the solution in terms of an integral. More precisely we find a function

G(x,t)
for which

L[y(x)]=a_{0}(x)y^{\prime\prime}(x)+a_{1}(x)y^{\prime}(x)+a_{2}(x)y(x)=-f(x),

(257)

with
0<x<1
and
y(0)=y(1)=0
and

y(x)=\int_{0}^{1}G(x,t)f(t)\ dt.

(258)

This function
G(x,t)
is the namesake Green’s function. We shall
obtaining via two usual methods. The first will make use of the Dirac-delta
function and the second of eigenfunction expansions.The general idea
is to proceed as in linear algebra, where if we want to solve a matrix
equation

A\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f},

(259)

we simply try to find the inverse operator
A^{-1}
, which is possible
as long as 0 is not an eigenvalue of
A
. We shall also see that
if
0
is not an eigenvalue of
L
, we shall also be able to construct
the Green’s function that provides the unique solution to
L[y]=f(x)
,
with appropriate boundary conditions.

F.2
Construction of Green’s function using the Dirac-delta function

We provide first some intuitive motivation for this approach. If we
let

L[y(x)]=a_{0}(x)y^{\prime\prime}(x)+a_{1}(x)y^{\prime}(x)+a_{2}(x)y(x)

(260)

We are trying to solve

L[y(x)]=-f(x),

(261)

for
t
such that
0<t<1
. Suppose there is a function
G(x,t)

for which

L[G(x,t)]=-\delta(x-t),

(262)

we can multiply both sides by
f(t)
and integrate with respect to

t
. Doing so, we have

\int_{0}^{1}\left\{L[G(x,t)]\right\}f(t)\ dt=-\int_{0}^{1}\delta(x-t)f(t)\ dt=-f(x).

(263)

If we can pull the operator
L
outside the integral, we have

L\left[\int_{0}^{1}G(x,t)f(t)\ dt\right]=-f(x),

(264)

and thus

y(x)=\int_{0}^{1}G(x,t)f(t)\ dt,

(265)

is a solution to the equation

L[y(x)]=-f(x).

(266)

Note that the previous considerations are sound, however we need a
way to construct the Green’s function. We first find
G(x,t)
such
that it satisfies the homogeneous equation for
x\neq t
.

L[G(x,t)]=0.

The solution will have two parts, one for
x<t
and
x>t
. If we
suppose that these solutions are
y_{1}(x)
and
y_{2}(x)
respectively.
Then the Green’s function can be written as

G(x,t)=\begin{cases}c_{1}(t)y_{1}(x)+c_{2}(t)y_{2}(x),&0\leq x<t\\
c_{3}(t)y_{1}(x)+c_{4}(t)y_{2}(x).&t<x\leq 1\end{cases}

(267)

We then need to find each
c_{i}(t)
and the Green’s function becomes
fully determined. To do this we use the boundary conditions

G(0,t)=G(1,t)=y(0)=y(1)=0,

(268)

along with continuity at
x=t
. This means that

\lim_{x\to t^{+}}G(x,t)=\lim_{x\to t^{-}}G(x,t).

(269)

Additionally, the derivative of the Green’s function can be shown
to have a discontinuity at
x=t
:

\lim_{x\to t^{+}}\frac{dG(x,t)}{dx}-\lim_{x\to t^{-}}\frac{dG(x,t)}{dx}=-\frac{1}{a_{0}(t)}.

(270)

The final condition is that it is symmetric and thus

G(x,t)=G(t,x).

(271)

From these boundary conditions one can obtain the coefficients
c_{i}(t)

and thus determine the Green’s function.

F.3
Construction of Green’s function from eigenfunctions

We continue to consider the operator

L[y(x)]=[p(x)y^{\prime}(x)]^{\prime}+q(x)y(x),

and want to solve the equation

L[y(x)]=-f(x),

with boundary conditions

y(0)=0,\ \ y(1)=0.

Suppose that
\left\{\phi_{n}\right\}
is a complete orthonormal
basis for the vector space consisting of eigenvectors of
L
that
satisfy the boundary conditions and that
\lambda_{n}
is the eigenvalue
of
\phi_{n}
. If we equip the vector space with the inner product

\left\langle f(x),g(x)\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}f(x)g(x)\ dx.

We then have

f(x)=\sum_{n}f_{n}\text{$\phi_{n}(x),\ \ y(x)=\sum_{n}y_{n}\phi_{n}(x)$,}

where

f_{n}=\left\langle f(x),\phi_{n}(x)\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\phi_{n}(x)\ dx,

(272)

and therefore we want to find the
y_{n}
’s to construct the solution.
Since
L
is a linear operator and
\phi_{n}(x)
is an eigenvector
of
L
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}
for each
n
we have that

L[y(x)]=L\left(\sum_{n}y_{n}\phi_{n}(x)\right)=\sum_{n}y_{n}\lambda_{n}\phi_{n}(x).

We get a relation for the coefficients since
L[y(x)]=-f(x)
and
therefore

\sum_{n}y_{n}\lambda_{n}\phi_{n}(x)=-\sum_{n}f_{n}\phi_{n}(x),

(273)

and since
\phi_{n}(x)
is a basis, we can equate the previous equation
term by term

y_{n}\lambda_{n}=-f_{n}.

(274)

Since
0
is not an eigenvalue, we can write

y_{n}=-\frac{f_{n}}{\lambda_{n}},

(275)

and thus

y(x)=-\sum_{n}\frac{f_{n}}{\lambda_{n}}\phi_{n}(x).

(276)

If we recall that
f_{n}=\left\langle f(x),\phi_{n}(x)\right\rangle
,
we have

\displaystyle y(x)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n}\frac{\phi_{n}(x)}{\lambda_{n}}f_{n}=-\sum_{n}\frac{\phi_{n}(x)}{\lambda_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}f(t)\phi_{n}(t)\ dt

(277)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}(t)}{\lambda_{n}}f(t)\ dt,

which, if we can exchange the summation and integral due to proper
convergence properties of the functions, we can write as

y(x)=-\int_{0}^{1}\left(\sum_{n}\frac{\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}(t)}{\lambda_{n}}\right)f(t)\ dt.

(278)

Thus, the Green’s function is

G(x,t)=-\sum_{n}\frac{\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}(t)}{\lambda_{n}}.

(279)

The crucial step to construct the Green’s function using this method
is thus to find the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions for
L
that satisfy
the initial conditions. Note that an alternative is to consider the
problem

\tilde{L}[y(x)]+\mu y(x)=\left[p(x)y^{\prime}(x)\right]^{\prime}+\tilde{q}(x)y(x)+\mu y(x)=-f(x),

(280)

where
\mu
is not an eigenvalue of
\tilde{L}
and thus, one obtains
the Green’s function

\tilde{G}(x,t)=-\sum_{n}\frac{\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}(t)}{\lambda_{n}-\mu}.

(281)

One can easily show, however that one form can be converted into another
by suitable renaming of
q(x)
and
\tilde{q}(x)
.

F.4
Dyadic Green’s functions using the Dirac-
\delta
- Example of a
single dipole

In this appendix we introduce the dyadic Green’s function as a natural
extension of the Green’s function discussed in the previous section
for the case of a vector equation. We focus on a critical and instructive
example of the wave equation for the electric field

\nabla\times\nabla\times\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)-\frac{\omega^{2}}{v^{2}}\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=i\omega\mu\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega).

(282)

We introduce the wave number
k^{2}=\omega^{2}/v^{2}
. The Green’s
tensor, or dyadic Green’s function
\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)

is defined through

\nabla\times\nabla\times\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)-\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)=\mathds{1}\delta(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}),

(283)

where
\mathds{1}
is the
3\times 3
unit matrix. Similarly to the
procedure followed in the previous section, we can multiply on both
sides by
i\omega\mu\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)

and integrate over the whole space.

\left[-\frac{\omega^{2}}{v^{2}}+\nabla\times\nabla\times\right]\left\{i\omega\mu\mu_{0}\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)\right\}=i\omega\mu\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega),

(284)

and thus this is a particular solution to the non-homogeneous equation.
If
\boldsymbol{E}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)
is a solution to the
homogeneous equation, the general solution is of the form

\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=\boldsymbol{E}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)+i\omega\mu\mu_{0}\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega).

(285)

To find the Green’s function we assume that the source of current
is a dipole at
\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{r}_{0}
, which means that

\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=-i\omega\boldsymbol{d}_{0}\delta(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}_{0})
.
The integral of the previous equation is thus immediate, and reads

\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=\boldsymbol{E}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)+\omega^{2}\mu\mu_{0}\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)\boldsymbol{d}_{0}.

(286)

In the Lorentz gauge, we can write the electric and magnetic fields
in terms of a scalar and vector potential
\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)

and
\phi(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)
. In this gauge we have

\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)&=i\omega\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)-\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{r},\omega),\\
\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)&=\nabla\times\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega).\end{cases}

(287)

If we substitute the electric field in the electric field equation,
we have

\displaystyle\nabla\times\nabla\times i\omega\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)-\nabla\times\nabla\times\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega^{2}}{v^{2}}\left[i\omega\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)-\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)\right]

\displaystyle=i\omega\mu\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega),

(288)

or

\displaystyle i\nabla\left[\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)\right]-i\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega}{v^{2}}\left[i\omega\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)-\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)\right]

\displaystyle=i\mu\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega),

(289)

and using the fact that
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=i\omega\mu\mu_{0}\varepsilon\varepsilon_{0}\phi(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=i\omega\phi(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)/v^{2}

we have

\displaystyle-\frac{\omega}{v^{2}}\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)-i\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)

\displaystyle-i\frac{\omega^{2}}{v^{2}}\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)+\frac{\omega}{v^{2}}\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)

\displaystyle=i\mu\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega),

(290)

where finally canceling the electric field term, we have

\left[\nabla^{2}+k^{2}\right]\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=-\mu\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{j}_{t}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega),

(291)

and if we replace the current with that produced by the dipole we
have

\left[\nabla^{2}+k^{2}\right]\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=-\mu\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{d}_{0}\delta(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}_{0}).

(292)

We get a differential equation for the components of the Green’s function
associated with the vector potential

\nabla^{2}g_{j}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r})+k^{2}g_{j}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)=i\omega\mu\mu_{0}d_{0}^{j}\delta(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}).

(293)

This equation can be solved using the Fourier transform. If we define

g_{j}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)=\int\frac{d^{3}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}g_{j}(\boldsymbol{p},\omega)e^{i\boldsymbol{p}\cdot(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})},

(294)

the differential equation (
293
) reduces to

g_{j}(\boldsymbol{p},\omega)=-\frac{i\omega\mu\mu_{0}d_{0}^{j}}{p^{2}-k^{2}}.

(295)

We can then make an inverse Fourier transform arriving at

\displaystyle g_{j}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)

\displaystyle=-i\omega\omega\mu\mu_{0}d_{0}^{j}\int\frac{d^{3}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{e^{i\boldsymbol{p}\cdot(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})}}{p^{2}-k^{2}}.

(296)

We can perform the integral by contour integration noting that there
is a pole at
p=\pm k
. We have

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{e^{i\boldsymbol{p}\cdot(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})}}{p^{2}-k^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}dp\ \frac{p^{2}e^{ip|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|\cos\theta}}{p^{2}-k^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dp\frac{p^{2}}{p^{2}-k^{2}}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta e^{ip|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|\cos\theta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dp\ \frac{ip\sin(p|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}|)}{\left(p^{2}-k^{2}\right)|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}.

The result allows us to write

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)+\omega^{2}\mu\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{d}_{0}\frac{e^{ik|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon\varepsilon_{0}}\nabla\left[\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{d}_{0}\frac{e^{ik|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}\right)\right].

(297)

Factoring the above expression we have

\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)=\boldsymbol{E}_{0}(\boldsymbol{r},\omega)+\mu\mu_{0}\omega^{2}\left(\mathds{1}+k^{-2}\nabla\nabla\cdot\right)\boldsymbol{d}_{0}\frac{e^{ik|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|},

(298)

which allows us to identify the dyadic Green’s function in real space
as

\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)=(\mathds{1}+k^{-2}\nabla\nabla)\frac{e^{ik|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}}{4\pi|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|},

(299)

where
\nabla\nabla=\nabla\otimes\nabla
is a dyadic differential
operator and
\otimes
is the tensor product. This serves as an example
of the computation of a dyadic Green’s function using from a Dirac-
\delta

function.

F.5
Finding the eigenmodes

In this appendix we begin the quantization of surface plasmons via
dyadic Green’s functions. Contrary to the previous section we shall
not use the construction from a delta function, but rather an expansion
of eigenfunctions (eigenmodes in this case).

The problem we are interested in solving consists of a graphene sheet
embedded between two semi-infinite dielectrics. As such, for
z>0

the dielectric function is a constant
\varepsilon_{1}
while for

z<0
the dielectric function is also constant and equal to
\varepsilon_{2}
.
At
z=0
we have a contribution from the graphene and therefore the
dielectric function over the whole space can be written as

\varepsilon(z)=\varepsilon_{1}\Theta(z)+i\frac{\sigma(\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega}\delta(z)+\varepsilon_{2}\Theta(-z),

(300)

where
\sigma(\omega)
is the conductivity of the graphene sheet.
In the Weyl gauge (see
Loffelholz et al. 2003
;
Haller 1987
;
Creutz 1979
)
we have that
\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r})=i\omega\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r})
,
where
\boldsymbol{E}(\boldsymbol{r})
is the electric field and

\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r})
is the vector potential. We thus
have a wave equation reading

-\nabla\times\nabla\times\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r})+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\varepsilon(z)\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{r})=0,

(301)

where we have assumed that there are no currents in the dielectric.
The graphene sheet is a boundary condition for this wave equation.
We will solve this equation via a dyadic Green’s function. It is such
that

-\nabla\times\nabla\times\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\varepsilon(z)\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=-\mathds{1}\delta(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}),

(302)

and our first goal is to construct this dyadic Green’s function. Our
approach will make use of an expansion in eigenmodes. We write the
Green’s function as

\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=\int d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}\sum_{n}\frac{\boldsymbol{a}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r})\otimes\left[\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})\right]^{*}}{N_{n}\lambda_{n}},

(303)

where
\lambda_{n}
are the eigenvalues,
N_{n}
a normalization
factor, and
\boldsymbol{a}_{n}
the eigenmodes, solutions to the
equation

-\nabla\times\nabla\times\boldsymbol{a}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r})+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\varepsilon(z)\boldsymbol{a}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r})=\varepsilon(z)\lambda_{n}\boldsymbol{a}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r}),

(304)

which we can write as

H(\boldsymbol{r})\boldsymbol{a}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r})=\varepsilon(z)\lambda_{n}\boldsymbol{a}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r}).

(305)

Since the system has dissipation

H(\boldsymbol{r})
is
not hermitian and the left and right eigenmodes are not the same.
The left eigenmodes are

\left[\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r})\right]^{*}H(\boldsymbol{r})=\varepsilon(z)\lambda_{n}\left[\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r})\right]^{*},

(306)

or

\left[H(\boldsymbol{r})\right]^{\dagger}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r})=\varepsilon(z)^{*}\lambda_{n}^{*}\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{r}).

(307)

The normalization factor can be found by evaluating

N_{n}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dz\ \varepsilon(z)[\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{n}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{r})]^{*}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{n}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{r}).

(308)

We assume
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and want
to solve for mode functions of the form

\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{a}_{n}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{r})=(a_{x}^{(1)},a_{y}^{(1)},a_{z}^{(1)})e^{i(q_{x}x+q_{y}y)}e^{-\kappa_{1}z}\Theta(z),\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{n}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{r})=(a_{x}^{(2)},a_{y}^{(2)},a_{z}^{(2)})e^{i(q_{x}x+q_{y}y)}e^{\kappa_{2}z}\Theta(-z),\end{cases}

(309)

where the
(1)
index shows that these propagate in the medium with

\varepsilon_{1}
while the index
(2)
shows that these propagate
in the medium with
\varepsilon_{2}
. This is the desired form for
our problem since we are interested in solutions to the equation that
propagate like waves along the graphene sheet and decay exponentially
as we go along into the dielectric media corresponding to the surface
plasmon polariton solutions. Substituting this form into equation
(
304
) one can easily check that it can be written
as a matrix equation

\begin{bmatrix}-q_{y}^{2}+\text{$\kappa$}_{1}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&q_{x}q_{y}&iq_{x}\kappa_{1}\\
q_{x}q_{y}&-q_{y}^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&iq_{y}\kappa_{1}\\
iq_{x}\kappa_{1}&iq_{y}\kappa_{1}&-q_{x}^{2}-q_{y}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}a_{x}^{(1)}\\
a_{y}^{(1)}\\
a_{z}^{(1)}\end{bmatrix}=\varepsilon_{1}\lambda_{n}\begin{bmatrix}a_{x}^{(1)}\\
a_{y}^{(1)}\\
a_{z}^{(1)}\end{bmatrix},

(310)

for
z>0
. We can solve this system for the eigenmodes and corresponding
eigenvalues. These are

\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{1}\lambda_{1}^{(1)}=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&\boldsymbol{a}_{1}^{(1)}=A_{1}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}e^{-\kappa_{1}z}(-iq_{x},-iq_{y},\kappa_{1})/\kappa_{1},\\
\varepsilon_{1}\lambda_{2}^{(1)}=\kappa_{1}^{2}-q^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&\boldsymbol{a}_{2}^{(1)}=A_{1}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}e^{-\kappa_{1}z}(-i\kappa_{1},0,q_{x})/q_{x},\\
\varepsilon_{1}\lambda_{3}^{(1)}=\kappa_{1}^{2}-q^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&\boldsymbol{a}_{3}^{(1)}=A_{1}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}e^{-\kappa_{1}z}(-q_{y},q_{x},0)/q_{x}.\end{cases}

(311)

The eigenvalues for the dielectric on the other side of the graphene
sheet is obtained by replacing
\kappa_{1}\to-\kappa_{2}
and
\varepsilon_{1}\to\varepsilon_{2}
.
We have, for this case

\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{2}\lambda_{1}^{(2)}=\frac{\varepsilon_{2}\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&\boldsymbol{a}_{1}^{(2)}=A_{2}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}e^{\kappa_{2}z}(iq_{x},iq_{y},\kappa_{2})/\kappa_{2},\\
\varepsilon_{2}\lambda_{2}^{(2)}=\kappa_{2}^{2}-q^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&\boldsymbol{a}_{2}^{(2)}=A_{2}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}e^{\kappa_{2}z}(i\kappa_{2},0,q_{x})/q_{x},\\
\varepsilon_{2}\lambda_{3}^{(2)}=\kappa_{2}^{2}-q^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&\boldsymbol{a}_{3}^{(2)}=A_{2}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}e^{\kappa_{2}z}(-q_{y},q_{x},0)/q_{x}.\end{cases}

(312)

An important observation, however, is that each eigenvalue with the
same index
n
on both sides of the graphene sheet must correspond
a single mode across the whole system. In symbols, this must mean
that
\lambda_{n}^{(1)}=\lambda_{n}^{(2)}
. This allows us to connect
the solution across the graphene sheet and relate
\kappa_{1}
with

\kappa_{2}
and
q
. We can do this by equating

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}-\frac{q^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}=\frac{\kappa_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{2}}-\frac{q^{2}}{\varepsilon_{2}}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}^{2}=q^{2}-\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}q^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}\kappa_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}^{2}=\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}\kappa_{1}^{2}+\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}\right)q^{2}.

(313)

Note however that the eigenvalues are degenerated for
n=2,3
. They
must indeed be superpositions of the
s-
and
p-
polarization
vectors

\begin{cases}\hat{e}_{s}=\frac{\boldsymbol{p}_{1}\times(0,0,1)}{q}=\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{q},-\frac{q_{y}}{q},0\right),\\
\hat{e}_{p}=\frac{\hat{e}_{s}\times\boldsymbol{p}_{1}}{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{p}_{1}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{1}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}\left(-i\frac{q_{x}\kappa_{1}}{q},-i\frac{q_{y}\kappa_{1}}{q},0\right),\end{cases}

(314)

where
\boldsymbol{p}_{1}=(q_{x},q_{y},i\kappa_{1})
. We can find
the
s-
and
p-
polarization components for
z<0
by writing

\kappa_{1}
as a function
\kappa_{2}
by using equation (
313
).
Note that if we substitute these polarizations into the matrix equation
(
310
) for the eigenmodes, we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-q_{y}^{2}+\text{$\kappa$}_{1}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&q_{x}q_{y}&iq_{x}\kappa_{1}\\
q_{x}q_{y}&-q_{y}^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}&iq_{y}\kappa_{1}\\
iq_{x}\kappa_{1}&iq_{y}\kappa_{1}&-q_{x}^{2}-q_{y}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{q_{x}}{q}\\
-\frac{q_{y}}{q}\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left(-q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}+\varepsilon_{1}\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\right)\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{q_{x}}{q}\\
-\frac{q_{y}}{q}\\
0\end{bmatrix},

(315)

and similarly for the
z<0
with the previously mentioned substitutions.
The same eigenvalue solves the equation for the
p-
polarization.
In particular, we want to solve only for plasmonic eigenmodes, which
only have
p-
polarization.

F.6
Boundary conditions - Dispersion relation in the electrostatic limit

We now proceed with connecting the solution for
z>0
with that for

z<0
. We have the usual boundary conditions for Maxwell’s equations.
For the electric field we have

\boldsymbol{E}_{\parallel}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{E}_{\parallel}^{(2)},

(316)

which we can translate into an equation for the eigenmodes since
\boldsymbol{E}_{\parallel}^{(j)}=i\omega\boldsymbol{a}_{\parallel}^{(j)}
.
Using then the
p-
polarization, which is parallel to the graphene
sheet at
z=0
, we have

\displaystyle A_{1}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}\left(-i\frac{q_{x}\kappa_{1}}{q},-i\frac{q_{y}\kappa_{1}}{q},0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{2}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}}}\left(i\frac{q_{x}\kappa_{2}}{q},i\frac{q_{y}\kappa_{2}}{q},0\right),

(317)

from where follows

\displaystyle-A_{1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}\kappa_{1}=A_{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}}}\kappa_{2}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle A_{2}=-A_{1}\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\kappa_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{q^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}}{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}.

(318)

The boundary condition for the magnetic field will take into account
the surface current that propagates along the graphene sheet. We can
write it as

\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}\times(\boldsymbol{B}_{\parallel}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{B}_{\parallel}^{(2)})=\mu_{0}\boldsymbol{K}=\mu_{0}\sigma(\omega)\boldsymbol{E}_{\parallel}^{(1)},

(319)

where we have used Ohm’s law. The magnetic field can be evaluated
as
\boldsymbol{B}_{\parallel}^{(j)}=\nabla\times\boldsymbol{a}_{\parallel}^{(j)}=i\boldsymbol{p}_{1}\times\boldsymbol{a}_{\parallel}^{(j)}
.
Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}_{\parallel}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=A_{1}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}i(q_{x},q_{y},i\kappa_{1})\times\left(\frac{q_{x}\kappa_{1}}{q},\frac{q_{y}\kappa_{1}}{q},0\right)

\displaystyle=A_{1}e^{i\left(q_{x}x+q_{y}y\right)}\frac{1}{q\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}\left(iq_{y}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{1}^{2}\right),-iq_{x}(q^{2}-\kappa_{1}^{2}),0\right),

(320)

and thus making the usual substitutions we find
\boldsymbol{B}_{\parallel}^{(2)}
.
Thus

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}\times(\boldsymbol{B}_{\parallel}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{B}_{\parallel}^{(2)})=

\displaystyle A_{1}e^{i(q_{x}x+q_{y}y)}\frac{1}{q\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}\left[\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{1}^{2}\right)+\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\kappa_{2}}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}\times\left(iq_{y},-iq_{x},0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{1}e^{i(q_{x}x+q_{y}y)}\frac{\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{1}^{2}\right)+\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\kappa_{2}}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2}\right)}{q\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}\left(iq_{x},iq_{y},0\right).

(321)

We have the additional constraints from the boundary condition

\displaystyle\left[\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{1}^{2}\right)+\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\kappa_{2}}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2}\right)\right]\left(iq_{x},iq_{y},0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\omega\mu_{0}\sigma(\omega)\left(-i\frac{q_{x}\kappa_{1}}{q},-i\frac{q_{y}\kappa_{1}}{q},0\right),

(322)

which yields

\left[\frac{1}{\kappa_{1}}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{1}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{\kappa_{2}}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2}\right)\right]=-i\omega\mu_{0}\sigma(\omega),

(323)

or rewriting

\kappa_{2}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{1}^{2}\right)+\kappa_{1}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2}\right)=-i\omega\mu_{0}\sigma(\omega)\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}.

(324)

and recalling the equation (
313
) that related
\kappa_{1}
,

\kappa_{2}
and
q
, we can write

\kappa_{2}^{2}=\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}\kappa_{1}^{2}+\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}\right)q^{2},

(325)

and solving for
q
, we have

q^{2}=\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}\right)\kappa_{2}^{2}-\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}\right)\kappa_{1}^{2},

(326)

and therefore

\displaystyle\lambda_{p}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=-q^{2}/\varepsilon_{1}+\kappa_{1}^{2}/\varepsilon_{1}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}+\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}+\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}+1\right)+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}+\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{1}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}\right)+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}}\right)(\kappa_{1}^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2})+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}.

(327)

The conductivity can be computed using the Drude model, with which
we have

\sigma(\omega)=\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\hbar}\frac{4E_{F}}{\Gamma-i\hbar\omega}\approx i\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\hbar}\frac{4E_{F}}{\hbar\omega},

(328)

where
E_{F}
is the Fermi energy and
\hbar\Gamma
is the momentum
relaxation rate. This corresponds to an approximation of the form

\sigma(\omega)\equiv\sigma_{1}(\omega)+i\sigma_{2}(\omega)\approx i\sigma_{2}(\omega).

The dispersion relation can be written as

\kappa_{2}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{1}^{2}\right)+\kappa_{1}\left(q^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2}\right)=\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{4E_{F}}{c^{2}\hbar^{2}}\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2},

(329)

and replacing
q^{2}
with the result of equation (
326
)
in this equation we have

(\kappa_{1}^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2})\frac{\kappa_{2}\varepsilon_{1}+\kappa_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}{\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}}=\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{4E_{F}}{c^{2}\hbar^{2}}\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2},

(330)

or in other words

(\kappa_{1}^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2})\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{\kappa_{1}}+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{\kappa_{2}}\right)=\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{4E_{F}}{c^{2}\hbar^{2}}\left(\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}\right),

(331)

or yet

(\kappa_{1}^{2}-\kappa_{2}^{2})=\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{4E_{F}}{c^{2}\hbar^{2}}\frac{\left(\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1}\right)}{\varepsilon_{1}/\kappa_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}/\kappa_{2}}.

(332)

This allows us to write the eigenvalues as

\lambda_{p}^{(1)}=\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}-\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{4E_{F}}{c^{2}\hbar^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{1}/\kappa_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}/\kappa_{2}},

(333)

and in the electrostatic approximation

\displaystyle\lambda_{p}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}-\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{4E_{F}}{\hbar^{2}c^{2}}\frac{q}{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}-\frac{4\alpha E_{F}c^{2}}{\hbar^{2}c^{2}}\frac{\hbar cq}{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}-\frac{2\alpha E_{F}}{\hbar c^{2}}\frac{cq}{\bar{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}-\frac{\omega_{\text{spp}}^{2}(q)}{c^{2}},

(334)

where
\bar{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})/2
and
where we have introduced the surface plasmon polariton (SPP) frequency
as

\omega_{\text{spp}}^{2}(q)\equiv\frac{2\alpha cE_{F}}{\hbar}\frac{q}{\bar{\varepsilon}}=\frac{q\omega\sigma_{2}(\omega)}{2\bar{\varepsilon}\varepsilon_{0}}.

(335)

The first form is valid for a Drude conductivity and the second in
general. The dispersion can therefore be found when
\omega=\omega_{\text{spp}},
or
in other words

\frac{q\omega\sigma_{2}(\omega)}{2\bar{\varepsilon}\varepsilon_{0}}=\omega^{2},

(336)

or rather still

\frac{\sigma_{2}(\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega}=\frac{2\bar{\varepsilon}}{q},

(337)

which also means that

i\frac{\sigma(\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega}\approx-\frac{2\bar{\varepsilon}}{q}.

(338)

This is an implicit equation for the spectrum of the SPPs, which may
be solved analytically or numerically depending on the complexity
of
\sigma(\omega)
.

F.7
Finding the normalization constant

In the previous appendix we have found the eigenmodes and eigenvalues,
as well as written them in a convenient form, relating
q
,
\kappa_{1}

and
\kappa_{2}
through the boundary conditions of our problem.
To find the Green’s function, in this appendix find the normalization
coefficient
N_{p}
. For the
p-
polarized modes we can evaluate
the normalization as

N_{p}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\varepsilon_{1}|A_{1}|^{2}e^{-2\kappa_{1}z}+\int_{-\infty}^{0}dz\varepsilon_{2}|A_{2}|^{2}e^{2\kappa_{2}z}+i\frac{\sigma(\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega},

(339)

where the first term covers
z>0
, the second
z<0
and the final
comes from the graphene boundary condition at
z=0
. We have, choosing

A_{1}=1

\displaystyle N_{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\varepsilon_{1}e^{-2\kappa_{1}z}+\int_{-\infty}^{0}dz\varepsilon_{2}\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}\frac{q^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}}{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}e^{2\kappa_{2}z}+i\frac{\sigma(\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2\kappa_{1}}+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{2\kappa_{2}}\frac{\kappa_{1}^{2}}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}\frac{q^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}}{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}+\frac{i\sigma(\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega},

(340)

which if we use that
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}
we can write
more simply as

N_{p}=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{\kappa_{1}}+\frac{i\sigma(\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega}=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{\kappa_{1}}-\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{4E_{F}}{\hbar^{2}\omega^{2}}.

(341)

In the electrostatic limit, we have
\kappa_{1}\approx\kappa_{2}\approx q

and thus we can write for the case when
\varepsilon_{1}\neq\varepsilon_{2}

N_{p}\approx\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2q}+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{2q}+i\frac{\sigma(\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega}=\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}}{q}+i\frac{\sigma(\omega)}{\varepsilon_{0}\omega}.

(342)

We can further write this result using the dispersion relation of
equation (
338
), and this way obtain

N_{p}\approx\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}}{q}-\frac{2\bar{\varepsilon}}{q}=-\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}}{q}

(343)

F.8
Construction of the dyadic Green’s function

In the present appendix we note that the dyadic Green’s function can
then be computed taking only into account the mode with
p-
polarization.
It reads

\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)\approx\int d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}\frac{\left[\boldsymbol{a}_{p}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{r})\right]^{T}\otimes\left[\boldsymbol{a}_{p}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})\right]^{*}}{N_{p}\lambda_{p}^{(1)}}.

(344)

In the limit
\kappa_{1}\approx q
the polarization vectors can be
written as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{a}_{p}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{q^{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}\left(-\frac{iq_{x}\kappa_{1}}{q},-\frac{iq_{y}\kappa_{1}}{q},q\right)e^{i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}}e^{-\kappa_{1}z}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{q\sqrt{2}}\left(-iq_{x},-iq_{y},q\right)e^{i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\rho}}e^{-qz},

(345)

where
\boldsymbol{\rho}=(x,y)
and thus, evaluating the tensor product
explicitly, and then substituting the forms of
N_{p}
and
\lambda_{p}^{(1)}
,
we have

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}\frac{e^{i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\rho})}e^{-q(z+z^{\prime})}}{2q^{2}N_{p}\lambda_{p}^{(1)}}\begin{pmatrix}-q_{x}^{2}&-q_{x}q_{y}&-iqq_{x}\\
-q_{x}q_{y}&-q_{y}^{2}&-iqq_{y}\\
-iqq_{x}&-iqq_{y}&q^{2}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\bar{\varepsilon}}\int d^{2}\boldsymbol{q}\frac{q}{q^{2}}\frac{c^{2}e^{i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\rho})}e^{-q(z+z^{\prime})}}{\omega^{2}-\left[\omega_{\text{spp}}(\boldsymbol{q})\right]^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}-q_{x}^{2}&-q_{x}q_{y}&-iqq_{x}\\
-q_{x}q_{y}&-q_{y}^{2}&-iqq_{y}\\
-iqq_{x}&-iqq_{y}&q^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(346)

The prefactor in the integral can be simplified. We introduce the
notation

C(q)=\frac{q}{q^{2}}\frac{c^{2}e^{-q(z+z^{\prime})}}{\omega^{2}-\left[\omega_{\text{spp}}(\boldsymbol{q})\right]^{2}}=\frac{1}{q}\frac{e^{-q(z+z^{\prime})}}{\omega^{2}/c^{2}-\frac{2\alpha E_{F}}{\bar{\varepsilon}\hbar c}q},

(347)

and this way, the integral can be written as

\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=-\frac{1}{2\bar{\varepsilon}}\int_{0}^{\infty}qdq\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\ C(q)e^{i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\rho})}\begin{pmatrix}-q_{x}^{2}&-q_{x}q_{y}&-iqq_{x}\\
-q_{x}q_{y}&-q_{y}^{2}&-iqq_{y}\\
-iqq_{x}&-iqq_{y}&q^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(348)

And defining
R\equiv|\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\rho}|
, we
have

\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=\frac{1}{2\bar{\varepsilon}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\ q^{3}C(q)e^{iqR\cos\left(\theta-\phi\right)}\begin{pmatrix}\cos^{2}\theta&\cos\theta\sin\theta&i\cos\theta\\
\cos\theta\sin\theta&\sin^{2}\theta&i\sin\theta\\
i\cos\theta&i\sin\theta&1\end{pmatrix},

(349)

where
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\rho}
makes an angle
\phi

with the
x-
axis. We therefore can evaluate the angular integrals

\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta e^{iqR\cos\left(\theta-\phi\right)}\times\begin{cases}1&=2\pi J_{0}(qR),\\
\cos^{2}\theta&=2\pi\left[J_{0}(qR)\cos^{2}\phi-\frac{J_{1}(qR)}{qR}\cos 2\phi\right],\\
\sin^{2}\theta&=2\pi\left[J_{0}(qR)\sin^{2}\phi+\frac{J_{1}(qR)}{qR}\cos 2\phi\right],\\
\cos\theta\sin\theta&=-2\pi J_{2}(qR)\cos\phi\sin\phi,\\
\cos\theta&=2\pi iJ_{1}(qR)\cos\phi,\\
\sin\theta&=2\pi iJ_{1}(qR)\sin\phi,\end{cases}

(350)

and now we have left to evaluate a series of integrals over the coordinate

q
.

F.9
Evaluation of the radial integrals

The angular integrals can be solved by adding a vanishing quantity
to the frequency, we will thus have, naming the matrix
\bar{\bar{\Theta}}(q,R,\phi)

containing the previously calculated terms involving the Bessel functions,

\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=\frac{c^{2}}{2\bar{\varepsilon}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dqq^{2}\frac{e^{-q(z+z^{\prime})}}{\left(\omega+i0^{+}\right)^{2}-\left[\omega_{\text{spp}}(\boldsymbol{q})\right]^{2}}\bar{\bar{\Theta}}(q,R,\phi),

(351)

where we have added a small part to the imaginary frequency, such
that we can use the Sokhotski–Plemelj identity (see appendix
E
)
to regularize the integral

\frac{1}{\omega^{2}-\left[\omega_{\text{spp}}(\boldsymbol{q})\right]^{2}+i0^{+}}=\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega^{2}-\left[\omega_{\text{spp}}(\boldsymbol{q})\right]^{2}}-i\pi\delta\left(\omega^{2}-\left[\omega_{\text{spp}}(\boldsymbol{q})\right]^{2}\right).

(352)

We note that we have previously seen that in the case of a Drude conductivity,

\omega_{\text{spp}}^{2}=A(\omega)q
. We now have to evaluate two
contributions to the integral, the first is the so called half-space
Hilbert transform, defined as

H(t)=\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\tau\frac{u(\tau)}{t-\tau}.

(353)

In our case, we will have integrals of the form

\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(1)}(\omega)=\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q(z+z^{\prime})}J_{\nu}(qR)}{\omega^{2}-A(\omega)q}.

(354)

It is useful to split this integral of a Bessel function into two
integrals involving the Hankel functions, such that

\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(1)}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2A(\omega)}\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q(z+z^{\prime})}\left[H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)+H_{\nu}^{(2)}(qR)\right]}{q_{\text{spp}}-q},

(355)

where we have introduced the SSP wave vector
q_{\text{ssp}}=\omega^{2}/A(\omega)
.
A useful result is that for large
qR
we can approximate the Hankel
functions as

\begin{cases}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)\approx\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi qR}}e^{i(qR-\nu\pi/2-\pi/4)},\\
H_{\nu}^{(2)}(qR)\approx\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi qR}}e^{-i(qR-\nu\pi/2-\pi/4)}.\end{cases}

(356)

The integral of the term in
H_{\nu}^{(i)}(qR)
can be performed
in the complex plane, over a contour
\gamma
such that

\oint_{\gamma}dz\frac{ze^{-z\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(i)}(zR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-z}=0,

(357)

and that the contour goes around the pole at
\omega^{2}/A(\omega)
.
Splitting the integral over the real line into the principal part
and the integral for
i=1
and
\nu=0,1
can be performed over the
small curve around the pole, and integrating over a quarter circle
of radius
\mathcal{R}
(see Fig.
8
)

Figure 8:
Contour
\gamma
over which we integrate equation (
357
)
for
i=1
and
\nu=0,1
. We integrate over a large quarter circle
of radius
\mathcal{R}
avoiding the pole with radius
\varepsilon
.
We will let
\mathcal{R}
go to infinity to obtain the desired integral.

We have, for
i=1

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\mathcal{R}}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}+\int_{\text{pole}}\frac{dqq^{2}e^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\pi/2}d\theta\frac{-\mathcal{R}^{2}e^{2i\theta}\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}e^{-\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}R)}{q_{\text{spp}}-\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}}+\int_{\mathcal{R}}^{0}\frac{idy\ -y^{2}e^{-2iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}

\displaystyle=0,

(358)

and thus, letting
\mathcal{R}\to\infty
, the integral over the arc
vanishes, and we are left with.

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}+\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\frac{i\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}{\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle-i\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\frac{y^{2}e^{-iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-iy}

\displaystyle=0,

(359)

where we can now solve for the desired half-space Hilbert transform

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\frac{i\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}{\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle+i\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\frac{y^{2}e^{-iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-iy}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\pi q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-\omega^{2}\zeta/A}H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle+i\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\frac{y^{2}e^{-iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-iy}.

(360)

We have a part which we were able to calculate analytically for all

R
, along with an integral we shall later approximate. For the second
Hankel function
H_{\nu}^{(2)}(qR)
, we must integrate over an analogous
contour in the fourth quadrant so that when we let the arc length

\mathcal{R}
go to infinity the corresponding integral vanishes
(see Fig.
9
).

Figure 9:
Contour
\gamma
over which we integrate equation (
357
)
for
i=2
and
\nu=0,1
. We integrate over a large quarter circle
of radius
\mathcal{R}
in the fourth quadrant, avoiding the pole
by moving along a semi-circle of radius
\varepsilon
around it.
We will let
\mathcal{R}
go to infinity to obtain the desired integral.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}+\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\pi}^{0}d\theta\frac{i\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}{\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{-\pi/2}d\theta\frac{-\mathcal{R}^{2}e^{2i\theta}\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}e^{-\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}(\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}R)}{q_{\text{spp}}-\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}}+\int_{-\infty}^{0}dy\frac{-iy^{2}e^{-iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}

\displaystyle=0,

which, proceeding as before, reduces to

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}=

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\frac{i\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}{\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle+i\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\frac{y^{2}e^{-iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-iy}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\pi q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}\left(\frac{\omega^{2}}{A}R\right)

\displaystyle+i\int_{-\infty}^{0}dy\frac{y^{2}e^{-iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-iy}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\pi q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle+i\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\frac{y^{2}e^{iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}(-iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}+iy},

(361)

We now substitute the asymptotic form of the Hankel function for large
arguments in the integral that is left. We find, in the limit
q(z+z^{\prime})\ll 1

and
qR\gg 1
, the second integral vanishes and we are left with

\begin{cases}\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-z\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}\approx-i\pi q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right),\\
\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-z\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}\approx i\pi q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right).\end{cases}

(362)

This means that we have the result

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(1)}(\omega)=\frac{1}{A(\omega)}\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q(z+z^{\prime})}J_{\nu}(qR)}{\omega^{2}-A(\omega)q}

\displaystyle\approx i\pi\frac{q_{\text{spp}}^{2}}{2A(\omega)}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}\left[H_{\nu}^{(2)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\pi\frac{q_{\text{spp}}^{2}}{A(\omega)}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}Y_{\nu}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right),

(363)

where
Y_{\nu}
is a Bessel function of the second kind. These results
are valid for
\nu=0,1
. For
\nu=2
we must also consider a pole
at the origin. The contour is thus a little bit different as we also
need to consider an additional small quarter turn to avoid the pole
at
z=0
(see Fig.
10
).

Figure 10:
Contour
\gamma
over which we integrate equation (
357
)
for
i=1
and
\nu=2
. We integrate over a large quarter circle
of radius
\mathcal{R}
in the first quadrant, avoiding the pole
at
q_{\text{spp}}
by moving along a semi-circle of radius
\varepsilon

around it. We also avoid the pole at
z=0
, by moving another quarter
circle of radius
\varepsilon
around it. We will let
\mathcal{R}

go to infinity to obtain the desired integral.

Here, our integral will read

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}=

\displaystyle-i\pi e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle+i\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\frac{y^{2}e^{iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-iy}

\displaystyle-\int_{pole_{0}}dq\frac{q^{2}}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}\left(-\frac{4i}{\pi q^{2}R^{2}}-\frac{i}{\pi}\right),

(364)

where we have expanded the Hankel function for small
qR
as

H_{2}^{(1)}(qR)\approx-\frac{4i}{\pi q^{2}R^{2}}-\frac{i}{\pi}.

(365)

The final term can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\int_{pole_{0}}dq\frac{q^{2}}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}\left(-\frac{4i}{\pi q^{2}R^{2}}-\frac{i}{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{pole_{0}}dq\frac{1}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}\left(-\frac{4i}{\pi R^{2}}-q^{2}\frac{i}{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}-\int_{0}^{\pi/2}i\varepsilon e^{i\theta}d\theta\frac{1}{q_{\text{spp}}-\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}\left(-\frac{4i}{\pi R^{2}}-\varepsilon e^{i\theta}\frac{i}{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{4}{\pi R^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{R^{2}},

(366)

thus, for
H_{2}^{(1)}(qR)
the integral contains an additional term.

\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q\zeta}H_{2}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}=-i\pi q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{2}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)+\frac{2}{R^{2}}.

(367)

The analogous result holds for
H_{2}^{(1)}(qR)
since we can expand
it as

H_{2}^{(2)}(qR)\approx+\frac{4i}{\pi q^{2}R^{2}}+\frac{i}{\pi},

(368)

and therefore have

\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q\zeta}H_{2}^{(2)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}=-i\pi q_{\text{spp}}^{2}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{2}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)+\frac{2}{R^{2}}

(369)

In this case we would have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(1)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{A(\omega)}\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q(z+z^{\prime})}J_{2}(qR)}{\omega^{2}-A(\omega)q},

\displaystyle\approx i\pi\frac{q_{\text{spp}}^{2}}{2A(\omega)}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}\left[H_{2}^{(2)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)+\frac{2}{R^{2}}-H_{2}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)+\frac{2}{R^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\pi\frac{q_{\text{spp}}^{2}}{A(\omega)}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}Y_{2}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)+\frac{2}{R^{2}}

\displaystyle=\pi\frac{q_{\text{spp}}^{2}}{A(\omega)}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}\left[Y_{2}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)+e^{q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}\frac{2A(\omega)}{\pi q_{\text{spp}}R^{2}}\right].

(370)

We have thus evaluated the principal value of the Sokhotski–Plemelj
identity, we have still to derive the
\delta-
function
part. This part is much simpler, however, since we have

\delta(\omega^{2}-A(\omega)q)=\frac{1}{A(\omega)}\delta(q-q_{\text{spp}}),

(371)

and thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}^{(1)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=-i\pi\frac{1}{A(\omega)}\int_{0}^{\infty}dqq^{2}e^{-q\zeta}J_{\nu}(qR)\delta(q-q_{\text{spp}})

\displaystyle=-i\pi\frac{q_{\text{spp}}^{2}}{A(\omega)}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}J_{\nu}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right).

(372)

The total radial integral is therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{(1)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(1)}(\omega)+\mathcal{I}_{2}^{(1)}(\omega)

\displaystyle\approx\pi\frac{q_{\text{spp}}^{2}}{A(\omega)}e^{-\omega^{2}\zeta/A(\omega)}\left[Y_{\nu}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{\nu}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right].

(373)

This is an approximate solution to the integrals of the form of equation
(
354
), but we still have to solve integrals of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(2)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{q^{2}e^{-q\zeta}\left[J_{\nu}(qR)\right]/qR}{\omega^{2}-A(\omega)q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{R}\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{qe^{-q\zeta}J_{\nu}(qR)}{\omega^{2}-A(\omega)q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{RA(\omega)}\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{qe^{-q\zeta}J_{\nu}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}.

(374)

We proceed in the same manner, splitting the Bessel function into
Hankel functions and integrating over contours in the complex plane.
We have, for the first Hankel function

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{qe^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}+\int_{\text{pole}}dq\frac{qe^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\pi/2}d\theta\frac{i\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}e^{-\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}R)}{q_{\text{spp}}-\mathcal{R}e^{i\theta}}-\int_{\infty}^{0}dy\frac{ye^{-iy}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}

\displaystyle=0.

(375)

Like before, the integral over the arc vanishes and we are left with

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{qe^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}=-

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\frac{i\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}{\varepsilon e^{i\theta}}q_{\text{spp}}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\frac{ye^{-iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-iy}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\pi q_{\text{spp}}e^{-\omega^{2}\zeta/A(\omega)}H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle-\int_{\infty}^{0}dy\frac{ye^{-iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-iy}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\pi q_{\text{spp}}e^{-\omega^{2}\zeta/A(\omega)}H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\frac{ye^{iy\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(iyR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-iy}.

(376)

Using the same considerations as in the first case, we are left with
the approximate result

\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{e^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}\approx-i\pi q_{\text{spp}}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(1)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right),

(377)

and similarly for
H_{\nu}^{(2)}(qR)
,

\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{e^{-q\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}(qR)}{q_{\text{spp}}-q}\approx i\pi q_{\text{spp}}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}H_{\nu}^{(2)}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right),

(378)

and finally for the delta function terms

\mathcal{I}_{2}^{(2)}(\omega)=-\frac{i\pi}{R}\int_{0}^{\infty}dqqe^{-q\zeta}J_{\nu}(qR)\delta(\omega^{2}-A(\omega)q)=-\frac{i\pi}{RA(\omega)}q_{\text{spp}}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}J_{\nu}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right).

(379)

We therefore can calculate the aforementioned integral as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{1}^{(2)}+\mathcal{I}_{2}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{RA(\omega)}q_{\text{spp}}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}\zeta}\left[Y_{\nu}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{\nu}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right].

(380)

Having calculated all these integrals, all we have left is to calculate
the Green’s tensor by putting together all the results. We shall do
this in the next section of this appendix.

F.10
Putting the results together

Here, we collect the results of the previous Appendices and note that
we now have all the results we need to construct the Green’s function.
It can be written as

\displaystyle\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=c^{2}\frac{\pi^{2}}{\bar{\varepsilon}}e^{-q_{\text{spp}}(z+z^{\prime})}\frac{q_{\text{spp}}^{2}}{A(\omega)}\bar{\bar{M}}(q_{\text{ssp}},R,\phi)

(381)

where the matrix
\bar{\bar{M}}(q,\phi)
has diagonal elements

\begin{cases}M_{11}=\left[Y_{0}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{0}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right]\cos^{2}\phi-\frac{1}{Rq_{\text{ssp}}}\left[Y_{1}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{1}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right]\cos 2\phi\\
M_{22}=\left[Y_{0}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{0}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right]\sin^{2}\phi+\frac{1}{Rq_{\text{ssp}}}\left[Y_{1}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{1}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right]\cos 2\phi\\
M_{33}=\left[Y_{0}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{0}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right]\end{cases}

(382)

and off-diagonal elements

\begin{cases}M_{12}=M_{21}=-\left[\left[Y_{2}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{2}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right]+\frac{2}{\pi q_{\text{spp}}R^{2}}e^{q_{\text{spp}}(z+z^{\prime})}\frac{A(\omega)}{q_{\text{spp}}}\right]\cos\phi\sin\phi\\
M_{13}=M_{31}=-\left[Y_{1}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{1}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right]\cos\phi\\
M_{23}=M_{32}=-\left[Y_{1}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)-iJ_{1}\left(q_{\text{spp}}R\right)\right]\sin\phi\end{cases},

(383)

where
A(\omega)
is defined as before by

\omega_{\text{spp}}^{2}=A(\omega)q

(384)

In the case of a Drude conductivity, this coefficient has the form

A(\omega)\approx\frac{2\alpha cE_{F}}{\hbar\bar{\varepsilon}},

(385)

but more generally, it is defined as

A(\omega)\equiv-i\frac{\omega\sigma(\omega)}{2\bar{\varepsilon}\varepsilon_{0}},

(386)

or if we let
\sigma(\omega)=\sigma_{1}(\omega)+i\sigma_{2}(\omega)
,
we can write more explicitly

\displaystyle A(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\bar{\varepsilon}\varepsilon_{0}}\left[-i\omega\sigma_{1}(\omega)+\omega\sigma_{2}(\omega)\right]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\omega\sigma_{2}(\omega)}{2\bar{\varepsilon}\varepsilon_{0}},

(387)

if only the imaginary part of the conductivity is considered.

F.11
Integrals of products of Green’s tensors

In this appendix we derive an important result which we shall use
quite often, regarding the integral of a product of Green’s tensors.
Let us consider

\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}(\boldsymbol{r},t)=\mu_{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega e^{-i\omega t}\int d\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}_{N}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\omega)+{\rm H.c.}

(388)

and

\hat{\boldsymbol{E}}(\boldsymbol{r},t)=-i\mu_{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu e^{i\nu t}\nu\int d\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}_{N}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\nu)\cdot\bar{\bar{G}}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\nu)+{\rm H.c.}

(389)

and let us make the commutator

\displaystyle[\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},t),-\epsilon_{0}\hat{\boldsymbol{E}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{2},t)]

\displaystyle=i\mu_{0}^{2}\epsilon_{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega e^{-i\omega t}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\nu e^{i\nu t}\nu\int d\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\int d\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime\prime}\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)[\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}_{N}(\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}},\omega),\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}_{N}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime\prime},\nu)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{r}_{2},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime\prime};\nu)]

\displaystyle=i\mu_{0}^{2}\epsilon_{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega e^{-i\omega t}{\cal C}\omega\sigma_{1}(\omega)\int d\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{r}_{2},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)

\displaystyle=i\mu_{0}^{2}\epsilon_{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega e^{-i\omega t}{\cal C}\omega\sigma_{1}(\omega)\int d\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r}_{2};\omega),

(390)

where we have used the reciprocity relation
[\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)]^{T}=\bar{\bar{G}}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r};\omega)

. To compute this integral we used Eq. (
423
)
. Let us now simplify the previous equation.

-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}}\times\nabla_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}}\times\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\epsilon(z)\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=-\mathds{1}\delta(\boldsymbol{r}_{1}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}),

(391)

We start with the Helmholtz equation and multiply from the left by

\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)

and integrating in
\boldsymbol{r}_{1}
it follows

\int d\boldsymbol{r}_{1}\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)[-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}}\times\nabla_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}}\times\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)]+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\int d\boldsymbol{r}_{1}\epsilon(z)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=-\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega).

(392)

Taking the complex conjugate of the Helmholtz equation (
391
)
we have

-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}}\times\nabla_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}}\times\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\epsilon^{\ast}(z)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}};\omega)=-\mathds{1}\delta(\boldsymbol{r}_{1}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}),

(393)

Multiplying from the left by
\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)

and integrating over
\boldsymbol{r}_{1}
yet again, we obtain

\int d\boldsymbol{r}_{1}\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\left[-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}}\times\nabla_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}}\times\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}};\omega)\right]+\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\int d\boldsymbol{r}_{1}\epsilon^{\ast}(z)\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}};\omega)=-\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega).

(394)

Making an integration by parts in the first term, we find after subtracting

\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\int d\boldsymbol{r}_{1}[-\epsilon^{\ast}(z)\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)+\epsilon(z)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)]=\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)-\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)

(395)

which can be written as

\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\int d\boldsymbol{r}_{1}[-\epsilon^{\ast}(z)\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)+\epsilon(z)\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r};\omega)]=2\textrm{Im}[\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)]

(396)

or

\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}\int d\boldsymbol{r}_{1}^{\prime}\textrm{Im}[\epsilon(z)]\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=\textrm{Im}[\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)].

(397)

where we have used the reciprocity relation and the property below

\displaystyle A_{ik}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\bar{\bar{G}}_{ij}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}_{jk}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)=\sum_{j}[\bar{\bar{G}}_{ji}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)]^{T}[\bar{\bar{G}}_{kj}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)]^{T},

(398)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}[\bar{\bar{G}}_{kj}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)]^{T}[\bar{\bar{G}}_{ji}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)]^{T}

(399)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow[\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime};\omega)]^{T}[\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)]^{T}=\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r}_{1};\omega)\bar{\bar{G}}^{\ast}(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r};\omega)

(400)

We also note the following property

G_{ij}(\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{r}},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})=G_{ji}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{r}})\Leftrightarrow\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{r}},\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})=[\bar{\bar{G}}(\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r})]^{T}.

(401)

Appendix G
Details of the quantization of the plasmon field in graphene

G.1
Transverse and longitudinal
\delta-
functions

We start this appendix with a small introduction to transverse and
longitudinal
\delta-
functions following
Milonni 2019
. Consider
the vector field

\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{r})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\nabla\times\nabla\times\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\frac{\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|},

(402)

obtained from another vector field
\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}).
We
may write this as

4\pi\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{r})=\nabla\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r})^{\prime}\cdot\nabla\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}+4\pi\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}),

(403)

where we have used that
\nabla\times\nabla\times\boldsymbol{C}=\nabla(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{C})-\nabla^{2}\boldsymbol{C}

and also that

\nabla^{2}\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}=-4\pi\delta(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}).

(404)

By the chain rule, we have

\displaystyle 4\pi\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{r})

\displaystyle=-\nabla\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r})^{\prime}\cdot\nabla^{\prime}\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}+4\pi\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r})

\displaystyle=\nabla\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\frac{\nabla^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}+4\pi\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}),

(405)

where in the last step we have integrated by parts and assumed that

\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r})\to 0
as
r\to\infty
. This way we
can write

\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r})=\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{r})-\frac{1}{4\pi}\nabla\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\frac{\nabla^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}.

(406)

Substituting the definition of
\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{r})
yet
again, we have

\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\nabla\times\nabla\times\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\frac{\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\nabla\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\frac{\nabla^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|},

(407)

and have thus decomposed the field
\boldsymbol{F}
into a longitudinal
and transverse part

\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}\equiv\frac{1}{4\pi}\nabla\times\nabla\times\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\frac{\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|},\\
\boldsymbol{F}^{\parallel}\equiv-\frac{1}{4\pi}\nabla\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\frac{\nabla^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})}{|\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}|},\end{cases}

(408)

since
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}=0
and
\nabla\times\boldsymbol{F}^{\parallel}=0
.
We can also obtain the longitudinal and transverse components of the
field by using

\begin{cases}F_{i}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{r})=\sum_{j}\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\delta_{ij}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})F_{j}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})\\
F_{i}^{\parallel}(\boldsymbol{r})=\sum_{j}\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}\delta_{ij}^{\parallel}(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})F_{j}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime})\end{cases},

(409)

where we have introduced the definition of the transverse and longitudinal

\delta-
functions as

\delta_{ij}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{r})\equiv\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{k}\left(\delta_{ij}-\frac{k_{i}k_{j}}{k^{2}}\right)e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}},

(410)

\delta_{ij}^{\parallel}(\boldsymbol{r})\equiv\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{k}\frac{k_{i}k_{j}}{k^{2}}e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}.

(411)

We have stated equation (
409
) without proof,
but one can be found, for instance in
Milonni 2019
. From these
definitions follows

\displaystyle\delta_{ij}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{r})+\delta_{ij}^{\parallel}(\boldsymbol{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int d^{3}\boldsymbol{k}\delta_{ij}e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\delta(\boldsymbol{r}),

(412)

and also, for each function by carrying out the integrals, that

\begin{cases}\delta_{ij}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{r})=\frac{2}{3}\delta_{ij}\delta(\boldsymbol{r})-\frac{1}{4\pi r^{3}}\left(\delta_{ij}-\frac{3r_{i}r_{j}}{r^{2}}\right),\\
\delta_{ij}^{\parallel}(\boldsymbol{r})=\frac{1}{3}\delta_{ij}\delta(\boldsymbol{r})+\frac{1}{4\pi r^{3}}\left(\delta_{ij}-\frac{3r_{i}r_{j}}{r^{2}}\right).\end{cases}

(413)

We can, as an example, carry out the first calculation, where we find
